# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-19

## Embodied AI
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[OxyGen: Unified KV Cache Management for VLA Inference under Multi-Task Parallelism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14371v5)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents increasingly require parallel execution of multiple tasks, such as manipulation, conversation, and memory construction, from shared observations under distinct time constraints. Recent Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) support such heterogeneous outputs, yet existing inference systems incur redundant computation and resource contention when serving concurrent tasks on-device. We identify isolated KV cache management as the root cause. To address this, we propose unified KV cache management, an inference design that treats the KV cache as a first-class shared resource across tasks and control cycles. This abstraction enables two key optimizations: cross-task KV sharing eliminates redundant prefill of shared observations, while cross-frame continuous batching batches unfinished language requests from different frames and advances them within a per-frame decoding budget. We implement this design in OxyGen and evaluate $π_{0.5}$ on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Thor. OxyGen achieves up to 3.7$\times$ speedup over isolated execution, delivering over 200 tokens/s language throughput and 70 Hz action frequency simultaneously on RTX 4090. We also validate the design on Xiaomi-Robotics-0 and StarVLA across different backbones and expert configurations. We further demonstrate the inference gains on a humanoid robot with on-board Jetson AGX Thor.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[RoboHarness: A Simple Harness Outperforms VLA and World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14018v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rise of large language models (LLMs) has opened a path toward generalist embodied AI, raising the question of how LLMs can act in the physical world. One straightforward approach is training the model to generate actions: collect robot-action data and video demonstration, refine LLM architecture with an action head, and train a vision-language-action (VLA) model at scale. However, we argue that coding agents have the potential to realize generalist embodied manipulation. All they need is a simple yet effective robot harness. We present RoboHarness: a robotic harness that gives LLM agents a visual-geometric control panel so that they can directly understand and invoke embodied tasks. With RoboHarness, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next outperforms state-of-the-art VLA models on BEHAVIOR Challenge 2025 tasks. The implementation is open source.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[Context Matters! Social, Spatial, and Semantic Coordination with an Embodied Agent in Immersive Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21484v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents in Augmented Reality (AR) could enable more natural collaboration in immersive analytics by engaging users through spatial, conversational, and visual interaction. However, designing such agents requires careful attention to context. We propose a preliminary conceptual framework for context-aware design of embodied agents structured around three dimensions of spatial, social, and semantic. This framework is further categorised into embeddedness (how agents are situated in the contexts) and awareness (how agents perceive and respond to the contexts). To explore this, we present Cobot, an embodied AI agent designed as a design probe. Cobot supports context-sensitive interaction through features such as gaze cues, spatial sound, and task-aware behaviour. We reflect on its design and identify implications for future contextaware embodied agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>In 20...</summary><p>In 2025 IEEE International Symposium on Mixed and Augmented Reality Adjunct (ISMAR-Adjunct) (pp. 189-194). IEEE</p></details> |
| **[PhyMix: Towards Physically Consistent Single-Image 3D Indoor Scene Generation with Implicit--Explicit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10125v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing methods for single-image 3D indoor scene generation often produce visually plausible results that violate real-world physical constraints, limiting their reliability in robotics, embodied AI, and virtual environment design. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Physics Evaluator that assesses four aspects (geometric priors, contact, stability, and deployability) through nine sub-constraints, establishing a benchmark for evaluating physical consistency. Our analysis reveals that even state-of-the-art methods produce scenes with substantial physical inconsistencies despite their visual plausibility. To address these limitations, we propose a framework that incorporates feedback from the Physics Evaluator into both training and inference to improve the physical plausibility of generated scenes. Specifically, PhyMix comprises two components: (i) implicit alignment via Scene-GRPO, a critic-free group-relative preference optimization strategy that uses physical evaluations to guide the generator toward physically plausible layouts, and (ii) explicit refinement via verifier-guided agentic repair, which translates evaluator diagnostics into executable scene-editing skills to correct residual violations at inference time. PhyMix unifies physical evaluation, preference-based alignment, and agentic inference-time correction to improve physical plausibility while retaining the observed scene content. Experiments show improvements in reconstruction quality and physical plausibility, while qualitative examples on stylized and real-world images illustrate the applicability of the framework beyond synthetic inputs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Plan-Bench: Can Existing VLMs Go Well Beyond Perception to Reason and Plan in Embodied Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18836v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in visual perception and spatial reasoning, leading to growing interest in using them as high-level planners for embodied AI. However, physical tasks require models to integrate spatial information, reason over temporal states, and generate multi-step plans under various constraints, raising the question of whether current VLMs can reliably support such reasoning and planning independently of execution. We introduce Plan-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating the high-level reasoning and multi-step planning capabilities of VLMs relevant to embodied worlds, while isolating the influence of action execution. It covers three complementary dimensions, spanning 16 subclasses and 699 curated samples. Extensive evaluation of 28 VLMs reveals room for improvement, with even the strongest model achieving only 75.0% accuracy. We further find that model scaling generally improves these capabilities. Ablation studies also indicate larger models show increasing potential as high-level planners. Overall, Plan-Bench provides a lightweight probe for evaluating VLMs' reasoning and planning capabilities relevant to embodied environments and assessing their potential as decision makers for physical tasks. Our benchmark and code are available at https://github.com/MinneFeng/Plan-Bench.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 11 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. The model performs multimodal unification at the representation level: appearance, geometry, and action are transformed into a common dynamic representation and jointly evolved by shared parameters, while their distinct output structures are preserved. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong performance across embodied world-model evaluations. Thinker-WM ranks first on EWMBench, WorldOlympiad and WorldArena, while controlled studies further demonstrate the benefits of action conditioning, geometric interaction, and multi-function training within the shared representation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://thinkercosmos.ubtrobot.com/thinker-wm</p></details> |
| **[Multi-View Relational Distillation for Spatial Reasoning with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10864v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong image and video understanding, yet their visual-spatial representations remain geometrically fragile, leading to failures in spatial reasoning needed for embodied AI, robotics, and autonomous driving. Prior approaches to geometry grounding either fine-tune VLMs on spatial question answering, which can perpetuate spurious visual representations, or fuse features from large geometry-grounded vision models, which substantially increases model size at inference. Knowledge distillation from geometry-grounded vision models offers an alternative, but directly matching multi-view teacher features can disrupt the pretrained alignment between visual and textual representations, degrading object- and language-semantic capabilities. We propose multi-view relational distillation (MVRD), which distills patch-wise cosine similarities across views instead of the teacher features themselves. These relations encode geometric correspondences adequate for spatial understanding, while leaving the student representation underdetermined, allowing it to remain close to its pretrained vision- language space. Across representative VLMs, MVRD improves visual-spatial reasoning, outperforming supervised fine-tuning and feature distillation while approaching feature fusion methods with considerably fewer added parameters and lower latency. We show that MVRD makes visual representations more geometric while retaining language alignment, and generalizes to 3D scene understanding tasks such as object grounding, dense captioning, and question answering.</p></details> |  |
| **[HelpMeOut: A Neuropsychology-Grounded Benchmark for Error-Aware Monitoring of Household Task Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17694v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce HelpMeOut, an embodied AI benchmark for error-aware monitoring of household task execution. Existing embodied household benchmarks typically assume that humans execute tasks correctly, but this assumption does not hold for cognitively impaired persons or older adults whose everyday activities produce structured execution errors. To address this gap, HelpMeOut operationalizes research on everyday action errors into 20000 paired activity episodes, covering nine neuropsychologically motivated error categories. In each episode, agents observe a streaming human activity trajectory and must monitor whether the execution is unfolding correctly, diagnosing any structured errors as early as possible. We evaluate learned-monitor, symbolic, change-point, and frontier LLM prompting baselines. Results show that these methods perform poorly as error monitors, often missing when execution goes wrong. These results suggest a gap left by standard goal-inference and task-completion benchmarks, making HelpMeOut a testbed for error-aware assistive agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026). 30 pages (9-page main text), 8 figures, 18 tables. Project page: https://whrcn.github.io/HelpMeOut-Official/</p></details> |
| **[ParallelWorld: Test-Time Scaling for Agentic Embodied Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22971v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI has emerged as a key paradigm for autonomous systems operating in complex physical environments. While end-to-end models have established strong local capabilities for perception and control, scaling these capabilities to extended, multi-stage tasks remains difficult. Agentic embodied systems address this challenge by introducing high-level planners that organize perception, reasoning, and executable skills over longer interaction horizons. However, existing agentic embodied frameworks typically generate actions for direct execution without explicitly simulating how alternative action sequences shape future observations and task progress, leading agents to commit to suboptimal interactions before considering their long-term consequences. To address this limitation, we propose ParallelWorld, an adaptive-horizon test-time scaling framework that grounds agentic embodied reasoning in the prospective simulation of action outcomes. Specifically, we design an agent system to simulate and evaluate alternative trajectories in parallel before committing to an action. A verifier assesses intermediate state transitions with respect to the task objective, dynamically pruning unpromising branches and guiding subsequent expansion. Based on the evaluated long-horizon outcomes, the agent selects a trajectory, executes only its first action, and replans as new observations become available. Experiments on ESI-Bench and LIBERO-Pro demonstrate improvements in active spatial reasoning and language-conditioned manipulation, respectively, highlighting the effectiveness of prospective planning for both understanding and manipulation tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://agenticei.github.io/ParallelWorld-page/ Code: https://github.com/AgenticEI/ParallelWorld</p></details> |
| **[Vision-Predictor-Action: Towards Efficient Embodied AI via Neuro-Symbolic JEPA Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15556v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models commonly generate robot actions token by token through Large Language Model (LLM) decoders, causing inference latency to grow with action sequence length and limiting closed-loop control frequency. We present Goal-Conditioned Vision-Predictor-Action (VPA), which replaces autoregressive decoders with a lightweight latent control loop. A shared vision encoder and a frozen text encoder embed observations, goal images, and instructions; a neuro-symbolic selector chooses a discrete skill; a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) predictor forecasts the next latent state; and a flow-matching solver generates an entire action chunk in a few integration steps, so that sequential network evaluations per decision step do not depend on the action horizon. We analyze inference complexity, regularization against representation collapse, and conditions under which latent sub-goal thresholding limits error accumulation across task phases. As an initial proof of concept, we evaluated VPA on the LIBERO-10 suite. The trainable model has under 20M parameters (checkpoint under 75 MB, excluding a frozen 63.4M-parameter CLIP text encoder); a decision step of a single-camera reference configuration takes 10-14 ms on an Apple M4; and trained only on LIBERO-10 demonstrations in under three hours on a single GPU, a two-camera policy succeeded in 40.0% of 200 evaluation episodes. These preliminary results from a single training run suggest the approach is workable. Code and weights are available at https://github.com/ylee-sling/Vision-Predictor-Action and https://huggingface.co/ylee-sling/vpa-libero10, and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22960920.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/ylee-sling/Vision-Predictor-Action | Checkpoints: https://huggingface.co/ylee-sling/vpa-libero10 | Zenodo: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22960920</p></details> |
| **[LERR-Nav: Learning to Coordinate Exploration, Recovery, and Reminiscing for Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15362v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object Navigation in unseen environments remains a fundamental challenge in embodied AI, as it requires agents to generalize visual and spatial reasoning to novel layouts. While recent advances in large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) have enhanced semantic mapping, spatial memory, and waypoint selecting for navigation, successful navigation requires more than selecting the next promising waypoint. A robot must also recognize stalled progress, escape unproductive behaviors, and revisit overlooked target cues. Addressing these challenges requires both specialized navigation strategies and a high-level policy that coordinates their execution. Motivated by the analysis of failed navigation episodes, we propose LERR-Nav, an object navigation framework that coordinates exploration, recovery, and reminiscing through a learned high-level policy. The framework comprises four complementary navigation strategies: value-function-guided exploration, LLM-guided exploration, recovery from stalled or repetitive behavior, and memory-guided reminiscing. To support history-aware coordination, we maintain the execution memory that preserves textual summaries of all strategy executions alongside a sliding window of map sequences. To learn the coordination policy, we initialize a high-level vision-language agent from Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct and train it through a two-stage curriculum. The first stage uses map-centric supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to establish spatial grounding. The second stage performs SFT on variable-length navigation segments to learn which navigation strategy to invoke next based on current maps and the hybrid execution memory. Experiments on HM3D and MP3D demonstrate state-of-the-art navigation performance, while ablation studies validate the contributions of individual components.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond LLM Serving: Characterizing Vision-Language-Action Workloads for Embodied AI System Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05062v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models translate multimodal observations into low-level robot actions. During robot operation, each control period sets an inference deadline, and overruns leave the robot acting on stale observations, reducing task success. Meeting this deadline motivates on-device or nearby edge execution, where a single robot requires batch-1 inference outside the design point of LLM serving systems. Although VLA architectures combine familiar vision-language, autoregressive, and diffusion-style components, their runtime behavior in this batch-1 control setting remains uncharacterized. We characterize four representative VLA models on an edge GPU server and two onboard SoCs, using single-inference profiling and 43,200 closed-loop episodes. Action tensor dimensionality determines whether a stage is memory- or compute-bound, platform balance can shift that bottleneck, and GPU frequency scaling yields a platform-dependent energy-latency sweet spot. In closed-loop operation, overlapping inference with action execution creates an accuracy-speed-energy tradeoff, and no configuration is Pareto-dominant across deployment SLOs. These results guide joint design of VLA model architectures, hardware, and runtime policies.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in the Proceedings of the 32nd ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS '27). 17 pages, 14 figures</p></details> |
| **[LIVIN: Benchmarking Spatial and Embodied Intelligence in Digital Twins of Lived-In Homes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12069v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic household simulation must capture not only diverse environments but also the lived-in object arrangements and spatial constraints that shape robot motion and interaction. Existing resources often trade off scale, real-world correspondence, and interaction readiness, leaving a gap in faithful, interactive replicas of how real homes are actually arranged. To this end, we introduce LIVIN, a benchmark for spatial and embodied intelligence built on digital twins of 30 diverse lived-in homes. These replicas preserve observed room layouts, furniture configurations, and everyday belongings. To construct them, we design a human-in-the-loop workflow comprising instance recognition, architectural reconstruction, and object generation and placement, with intermediate results reviewed and corrected by humans against the source observations at each stage. We evaluate four tasks in LIVIN: 3D detection, 3D reconstruction, navigation, and loco-manipulation. Our evaluations show that current methods remain challenged by the dense object arrangements, occlusions, limited free space, and constrained interaction regions found in realistic lived-in homes. We hope LIVIN will help advance embodied AI in real-world homes, from spatial understanding to robotic interaction, and ultimately bring embodied intelligence into everyday home environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[USER: A Unified and Extensible System for Real-World Online Policy Learning in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07837v5)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online policy learning directly in the physical world is a promising yet challenging direction for embodied intelligence. Unlike simulation, real-world systems cannot be arbitrarily accelerated, cheaply reset, or massively replicated, suggesting that real-world policy learning is not merely an algorithmic problem, but inherently a systems problem. We present USER, a \underline{U}nified and extensible \underline{S}yst\underline{E}m for real-world online policy lea\underline{R}ning. On the systems side, USER introduces a hardware abstraction layer for unified robot management and an adaptive communication plane that enables efficient cloud-edge training. On the learning side, USER adopts a fully asynchronous training framework, designs a persistent and cache-aware replay buffer, and provides extensible abstractions for rewards, algorithms, and policies. Experiments in both simulation and the real world demonstrate that USER supports multi-robot coordination, heterogeneous manipulators, cloud-edge training with large models, and long-running asynchronous training. Together, these capabilities establish USER as a unified and extensible systems foundation for real-world online policy learning.</p></details> | Accepted to RSS 2026 |
| **[OmniDex: Scaling Dexterous Hand Grasping to Diverse Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11194v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous grasping is the foundational primitive in embodied AI, demanding massive data to train robust models. As real-world data collection is expensive, simulation has become the mainstream paradigm. Yet, while cluttered scenes best reflect real-world applications, learning to grasp within them is bottlenecked by a critical scarcity of large-scale data. To resolve this, we curate high-quality 3D objects and supporting bases, proposing a scalable seed-and-filter strategy that bypasses sluggish scene-level optimization. This yields an unprecedented benchmark comprising over 2.6 million scenes and 0.4B scene-specific grasp ground truths, featuring diverse realistic layouts paired with rich semantic and geometric observations. Furthermore, we introduce the OmniDex model to overcome the grasp multimodality and last-millimeter precision errors plaguing current generative models. By coupling Soft Winner-Takes-All learning with human-inspired physical constraints during training, and utilizing physics-driven ranking, our approach achieves robust dexterous grasping without the latency of post-optimization. Experimental results show that OmniDex model achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across diverse scenes, views, and unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generative AI for Autonomous Driving: Frontiers and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08854v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) constitutes a transformative technological wave that reconfigures industries through its unparalleled capabilities for content creation, reasoning, planning, and multimodal understanding. This revolutionary force offers the most promising path yet toward solving one of engineering's grandest challenges: achieving reliable, fully autonomous driving, particularly the pursuit of Level 5 autonomy. This survey delivers a comprehensive and critical synthesis of the emerging role of GenAI across the autonomous driving stack. We begin by distilling the principles and trade-offs of modern generative modeling, encompassing VAEs, GANs, Diffusion Models, and Large Language Models (LLMs). We then map their frontier applications in image, LiDAR, trajectory, occupancy, video generation as well as LLM-guided reasoning and decision making. We categorize practical applications, such as synthetic data workflows, end-to-end driving strategies, high-fidelity digital twin systems, smart transportation networks, and cross-domain transfer to embodied AI. We identify key obstacles and possibilities such as comprehensive generalization across rare cases, evaluation and safety checks, budget-limited implementation, regulatory compliance, ethical concerns, and environmental effects, while proposing research plans across theoretical assurances, trust metrics, transport integration, and socio-technical influence. By unifying these threads, the survey provides a forward-looking reference for researchers, engineers, and policymakers navigating the convergence of generative AI and advanced autonomous mobility. An actively maintained repository of cited works is available at https://github.com/taco-group/GenAI4AD.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACM Computer Survey</p></details> |
| **[World Models' Last Exam in Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08791v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video world models can produce visually convincing yet physically inconsistent sequences, raising concerns about their reliability for prediction and planning in embodied AI systems. Existing evaluations often rely on model-based judgments or reference videos, while direct physical tests largely focus on mechanics. We introduce World Models' Last Exam in Physics, a measurement-based benchmark for evaluating physical consistency in video world models. The benchmark comprises 40 controlled tasks spanning mechanics, optics, fluids, thermal and phase-change phenomena, electromagnetism, and surface tension. Each task pairs an initial image and a generation prompt with predefined physical criteria, enabling interpretable tests of observable physical relationships without requiring reference videos. Its evaluator combines task-observability screening with task-specific quantitative physical measurements. Experiments on eight video generation models across 1,280 videos reveal persistent physical inconsistencies and substantial variation across tasks, with the best model achieving an overall score of 57.76 out of 100. Evaluation on synthetic videos with known physical relationships provides evidence for the validity of the measurement module under controlled conditions. The evaluator also achieves higher agreement with human judgments than a direct vision-language model baseline in both within-task rankings and pairwise comparisons. By combining coverage across physical domains with scores grounded in measurable evidence and explicit measurement limitations, the benchmark provides an interpretable basis for diagnosing physical inconsistencies and tracking progress toward physically consistent video world models.</p></details> |  |
| **[WorldSolver: Can LLM Agents Simulate the Physical Dynamics via Solver Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08720v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents are increasingly advancing scientific and engineering problem solving, with physics simulation emerging as a challenging yet practical testbed for reproducing complex physical phenomena with application in embodied AI, games and films. As the workhorse of such simulation, a solver computes how the state of a dynamic system evolves over time. Building such solvers requires physical understanding to identify appropriate models, mathematical reasoning to formulate the underlying dynamics, and software engineering to implement them as executable code, yet this capability of LLM agents remains underexplored. To this end, we introduce WorldSolver, a benchmark of 168 simulation tasks derived from physical phenomena in 61 classic computer graphics papers, spanning 7 physical domains. Each task contains a code scaffold that provides a fixed simulation environment for the scene, with the solver implementation left for the agent to complete. Specifically, we evaluate them along three dimensions: Execution Checks for successful execution, Visual Fidelity for reproducing the intended dynamic behavior in the rendered simulation, and Physical Plausibility for physics-grounded verification of the generated dynamics. Experiments on frontier agents reveal that producing executable solvers is difficult itself, and satisfying visual and physical correctness is even harder. GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5 perform comparatively better than the other evaluated agents, yet achieve overall scores of only 48.7% and 46.7%, respectively. WorldSolver is an early step toward agentic solver generation, and we hope it helps drive progress toward agents that can faithfully simulate the dynamic physical world. Code is available at https://github.com/sirujiang/WorldSolver.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is this machine playing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07130v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed a modern AI coding assistant in an unintended role: as the mind of a body on an unknown digital island. With only a minimal instruction mentioning no specific task, reward, or activity, the machine started animating its virtual body. Across thirty-hour runs, the embodied AI agent climbed hills, stacked blocks into towers, drew mandalas, reinterpreted sports, ran experiments on the physics of its world, and learned techniques that later expanded what it could accomplish. These activities recurred across thirteen agents but diverged into distinct histories. We examine whether this behavior satisfies classical criteria for play and ask whether play can become a mode of machine development.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages of main text, 17 figures</p></details> |
| **[Look-Before-Move: Narrative-Grounded World Visual Attention in Dynamic 3D Story Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26964v3)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As embodied AI and world models increasingly operate in dynamic 3D environments, visual perception must move beyond passively interpreting given observations toward actively deciding what to observe. We study this problem through camera planning in dynamic 3D story worlds, where the camera must not only generate smooth motion, but also decide what visual evidence should be acquired before it moves. We formulate this capability as Narrative-Grounded World Visual Attention, where the camera acts as an embodied observer that determines what to observe, how to compose the observation, and how to shift attention over time under narrative intent and physical 3D constraints. To realize this capability, we propose Look-Before-Move, a camera planning framework that separates observation specification from motion execution. It first builds a Semantic Observation Contract to convert directorial intent into executable visual constraints, then performs Monte Carlo Viewpoint Search to find narrative-compliant and geometrically feasible viewpoints, and finally applies Semantic Trajectory Grounding to connect selected viewpoints into continuous, collision-aware, and temporally coherent camera motion. We further construct a dynamic 3D Story World Benchmark based on StoryBlender, covering 50 stories, 457 scenes, and 1585 shots with animated characters, semantic scene configurations, and executable 3D environments. Experiments show that our framework improves subject perception, intent consistency, and trajectory quality over representative baselines, demonstrating the importance of organizing visual attention before generating camera motion.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track, Poster). 30 pages (including references and appendices), 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[Inspect Robots: Evaluating the Capabilities and Safety of Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06306v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General purpose language models are increasingly able to control robotic hardware. Understanding the capabilities and safety of these models when embodied is therefore increasingly important for understanding their societal impact and risks. To this end, we introduce Inspect Robots, a modular, open-source framework for developing and running evaluations of embodied agents. Inspect Robots pairs customizable, reusable abstractions for specifying physical evaluations and analyzing their results with infrastructure that automates evaluation setup, execution and termination. We demonstrate Inspect Robots by using it to evaluate the capabilities and safety of six policies based on frontier language models. Inspect Robots has seen significant early uptake, receiving nearly 100,000 downloads in the three months since its release.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to The Science of Physical AI Safety (SPAIS) Workshop at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Survey of Robotic Navigation and Manipulation with Physics Simulators in the Era of Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01458v3)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Navigation and manipulation are core capabilities in Embodied AI, but training agents to perform them directly in the real world is costly, time-consuming, and unsafe. Therefore, sim-to-real transfer has emerged as a key approach, yet the sim-to-real gap persists. This survey examines how physics simulators address this gap by analyzing properties that have received limited attention in prior surveys. We also analyze their features for navigation and manipulation tasks, as well as their hardware requirements. Additionally, we offer a resource with benchmark datasets, metrics, simulation platforms, and methods to help researchers select suitable tools while accounting for hardware constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages, 9 figures. Accepted to ACM Computing Surveys (CSUR)</p></details> |
| **[EnvDreamer: Large-Scale Multimodal-to-Environment Generation for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04301v1)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large datasets and high capacity models have accelerated progress in vision and language. This work introduces a platform aimed at bringing comparable gains to embodied learning, world models, and robotics. We present EnvDreamer, a framework that uses large language and vision language models to generate Unreal Engine 5 environments for embodied AI and robot training. EnvDreamer enables sampling of large, diverse, interactive, customizable, and validator passed virtual environments for training and evaluation across navigation, interaction, and manipulation. We illustrate the platform with a large set of generated scenes and simple baselines. Policies trained on EnvDreamer generated environments, without explicit mapping or human task supervision, achieve competitive results on multiple embodied benchmarks spanning navigation, rearrangement, and manipulation. EnvDreamer also supports image-conditioned reconstruction for real-to-sim studies. Finally, we release EnvDreamer-20k, a dataset of 20,000 validator passed environments with task programs, scene graphs, trajectories, and metadata to support reproducible benchmarking.</p></details> |  |
| **[From bits to atoms: A survey of cross-layer safety in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14038v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied Artificial Intelligence (AI) introduces cyber-physical risks because digital failures and attacks can cause physical harm. This survey organizes embodied AI safety through a four-layer reference architecture -- comprising the physical interaction, system and middleware, algorithm and decision, and cloud-edge and data layers -- and uses it to connect vulnerabilities, propagation paths, and safeguards across the system stack. It traces the evolution of safety paradigms and examines how faults and attacks cross architectural boundaries to produce physical consequences. Furthermore, the survey systematizes composite attack chains, outlines multi-layered defense strategies, and reviews representative embodied safety benchmarks. Ultimately, we advocate for unifying software alignment with rigorous hardware constraints to build trustworthy systems capable of operating safely within human-centric environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Revie...</summary><p>Review article published in AI Plus. 25 pages, 6 figures. URL: https://www.the-innovation.org/ai-plus/article/doi/10.59717/ipj.aiplus.2026.100013</p></details> |
| **[A Token Service Interface for AI-Native RANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02618v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative and embodied AI services exchange token streams within continuing inference and control loops. We further introduce pragmatic value of information (VoI) and pragmatic cost of information (CoI) as decision-theoretic duals to rate-distortion and capacity, and develop a Lagrangian dual framework for cross-layer optimization. | **[Valerant: An Automatic Navigable Game Map Generator via Action-Conditioned World Model Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09418v3)** | 2026-09-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World Action Models (WAMs) couple predictive world modeling with action generation, allowing anticipated future states to guide agent behavior. | **[When Robots Mishear Us: Mapping the Safety Risks of Voice-Controlled Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28518v1)** | 2026-08-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We investigate whether automatic speech recognition (ASR) errors in user input can lead to unsafe outputs from Embodied AI (EAI) models. ## Reinforcement Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Coordination Matters: Process-Aware Evaluation of Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06557v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) benchmarks commonly emphasize aggregate outcomes such as return, success rate, or completion time. While essential, these metrics can obscure how agents coordinate and fail to distinguish policies that achieve similar outcomes through different interaction processes. We propose a process-aware evaluation framework that complements aggregate performance with diagnostics of interference, coordinated task completion, participation, and coordination efficiency. We evaluate the framework in STAT, a controlled spatial task-allocation testbed, and examine its broader applicability in two established cooperative MARL benchmarks. Across diverse value-based and actor-critic methods, we find policies with nearly identical return can exhibit substantial differences in coordination behavior. Controlled scaling in STAT reveals distinct process signatures for spatial, task, and agent scaling, while a reward-shaping ablation shows that explicitly penalizing assignment conflicts can alter coordination behavior with little change in task return. Results in the established benchmarks further show that the framework remains informative under different coordination mechanisms. These findings support process-aware evaluation as a complement to outcome-based benchmarking. The framework provides a common structure for selecting and interpreting environment-specific diagnostics that characterize not only what cooperative policies achieve, but how they coordinate to achieve it.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[Blind Precise Assembly: Proprioceptive Sim-to-Real Peg-in-Hole for Diverse Parts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23849v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every robot already carries high-resolution contact sensors: its own joints. Legged robots and humanoids exploit them to walk, run, and track whole-body motions without vision, but there the contacts are large and forceful. Is joint sensing also enough for precise, contact-rich assembly, where the robot must find a millimeter-scale opening, mesh gear teeth, or catch a thread through light contact? We study this question by training blind assembly policies that observe only a short history of joint positions, velocities, and their own actions, without visual, force, torque, or tactile sensing. The poses of both the fixture and the part are unknown to the policy, and the grasp is randomized substantially. The policy must therefore assemble the parts through contact, sensed exclusively through its own proprioception. Trained with reinforcement learning entirely in simulation over randomized parts, grasps, and contacts, such policies learn peg insertion, gear meshing, and nut threading. The peg insertion policy transfers zero-shot to the real world and inserts pegs of different shapes and sizes in 73% of trials. Controlled comparisons with matched visual policies in simulation show that blind policies generalize far better to unseen part shapes and sizes, though they can be slower. Training such policies needs thousands of different assemblies, each simulated with its clearance intact, so we also release an open-source generator that builds them procedurally from exact convex pieces for MuJoCo; it is more accurate and faster than convex decomposition or MuJoCo's signed-distance collision. Videos and interactive simulations are at https://younghyopark.me/blind-proprioceptive-assembly/.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 15 figures, 7 tables. Antonia Bronars and Younghyo Park contributed equally. Project page: https://younghyopark.me/blind-proprioceptive-assembly/</p></details> |

Enlightened by this progress, we propose a novel diffusion-based approach for symbolic regression. In addition, we introduce a long short-term risk-seeking policy to expand the pool of top-performing candidates, further enhancing performance. Extensive experiments and ablation studies have demonstrated the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> |  |

We therefore formulate RNA inverse design as a topology-and-geometry co-design problem and propose TANGO, a multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for this task. TANGO adopts a staged curriculum: it first learns a topology-aware policy from secondary-structure targets, then transfers this policy to tertiary-structure targets to train a general co-design model, and finally applies frontier-guided fine-tuning to refine target-specific trade-offs among secondary-structure fidelity, tertiary-structure fidelity, and sequence diversity. | **[CocoRNA: Collective RNA Design with Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23820v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing RNA sequences that reliably fold into specific secondary structures is essential for understanding their biological functions but remains a challenging computational problem. | **[Not All Actions Contribute Equally: Learning Precise Progress Estimation from Imprecise Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23769v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Progress estimators play an essential role in providing dense reward signals for reinforcement learning in robotic manipulation, but accurately assessing task progress can be much challenging. Existing methods heavily rely on expert data and naively assume that all actions within a demonstration contribute to task success. Therefore, these methods can erroneously interpret human mistakes as meaningful progress, further corrupting progress learning. We argue that a key limitation is the lack of a global prior regarding task evaluation criteria, which is crucial for distinguishing meaningful progress from human mistakes. To this end, we propose PRIME, a novel progress estimator capable of learning precise progress estimation from imprecise demonstrations by aggregating experience across trajectories. PRIME constructs a weighted graph over observed states, connecting states across trajectories based on spatial proximity and visual consistency. Edge weights encode spatial distances as transition costs, and shortest-path search approximates the geodesic distance to success. Changes in this distance naturally distinguish advancement, stagnation, and setbacks without assuming monotonic progress over time. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real world on precise manipulation tasks show that PRIME is consistently effective across diverse tasks, and robust to data with varying degrees of suboptimality. Notably, the advantage of PRIME gets more pronounced in higher-precision scenarios, yielding up to a \(5\times\) improvement in policy success rate over the SFT baselines on charger insertion.</p></details> | 9 pages, 13 figures |

| **[When Is Language-Model Advice Worth Buying? On BabyAI GoToObj with Qwen2.5-1.5B and 7B advisors, the gate improves return over no advice by paired $0.029\pm0.016$ and $0.030\pm0.015$ while using more than $97\%$ fewer calls than always asking. Groupwise calibration raises coverage on positive-value opportunities from $0.47$ to $0.85$, still below the $0.90$ target. In a typical implementation, learning is off-policy: The policy generating the responses differs from the policy being trained because of training-inference mismatch or policy staleness in asynchronous training. This discrepancy is commonly addressed by importance sampling, whose ratio is usually regularized in practice through truncation, masking, or clipping. Under regularized importance sampling, the importance-weighted policy gradient is no longer invariant to constant shifts in the reward. This invariance is recovered by using the covariance between the reward and the importance-weighted score as the estimand, which coincides with the policy gradient when the ratio is not regularized. This note develops this covariance perspective for both sequence-level and token-level importance sampling. The covariance perspective reveals the roles of the group-mean baseline and score centering in policy gradient estimation. Both perform centering in covariance estimation, but they differ in the mean being subtracted: The group-mean baseline uses a sample mean, whereas score centering uses a population mean. The two lead to distinct unbiased covariance estimators with different variances. Whether both forms of centering should be used simultaneously depends on whether importance sampling is applied at the sequence level or at the token level. | **[BRIDGE: Bridging Reasoning In Distillation Gap Elimination via Structure-Aware Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17686v7)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly improved LLMs' mathematical problem-solving capabilities, but distilling such capabilities into smaller models remains challenging due to the capacity mismatch between verbose teachers and compact students. Directly copying teachers' lengthy reasoning chains causes capacity overload, resulting in truncated outputs or repetitive failure. Existing remedies each sacrifice a critical property of CoT: implicit reasoning methods (e.g., compressing reasoning into hidden states) trade away interpretability and verifiability, while heuristic compression strategies (e.g., random step pruning) destroy logical integrity. To address this, we propose BRIDGE, a curriculum framework that first establishes structural understanding via masked reconstruction, then uses GRPO-based reinforcement learning to guide students in self-discovering the optimal balance between accuracy and brevity, and finally internalizes complex reasoning through teacher-guided rewriting on failure cases. On GSM8K, BRIDGE enables Qwen2.5-3B to achieve 11.29% accuracy improvement and 27.4% token reduction over the original model, outperforming instruction-tuned variants and distillation baselines. Zero-shot transfer experiments on SVAMP and MATH-500 further confirm the generalization of internalized reasoning. Our code and model checkpoints are publicly available at https://github.com/Applied-Machine-Learning-Lab/SDM2026_BRIDGE and https://huggingface.co/bowen0815/BRIDGE.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 13 figures. Accepted at SDM 2026</p></details> |

## Robotics
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[MOSS: Multimodal Origami Swamp Robot System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23813v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic systems for environmental monitoring and ecological surveying in wetlands and swamps must operate across diverse terrestrial and aquatic conditions. Conventional fixed-geometry wheels cannot adapt their contact geometry to these varying conditions. This work presents MOSS (Multimodal Origami Swamp Robot System), a compact amphibious robotic platform with 3D-printed origami wheels. The compact crank mechanism switches the wheel between contracted and expanded configurations, changing its width, body diameter, and fin orientation simultaneously. The contracted configuration is better suited to efficient rolling on firmer ground, whereas the expanded configuration provides greater contact and terrain engagement on softer surfaces. MOSS was evaluated on soil, sand, mud with varying water content, and water, in addition to in-motion morphing and terrestrial-aquatic transition tests. The more energy-efficient wheel configuration depended on terrain. The contracted configuration achieved a lower cost of transport on firmer soil and mud, whereas the expanded configuration performed better on wetter mud, sand, and water, reducing the cost of transport by up to 29%. Expanded wheels also reduced slip in wet mud and reduced both slip and hub sinkage in sand. MOSS successfully completed configuration changes in $1.95 \pm 0.11$ s and demonstrated continuous locomotion across soil-mud-water transitions. Outdoor trials further demonstrated operation across natural deformable terrain and water. These results show that adaptive wheel geometry provides terrain-dependent locomotion benefits and enables a single compact platform to traverse substantially different terrestrial and aquatic conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 8 figures, submitted to ICRA 2027</p></details> |
| **[Learning Robot Metareasoning: Adaptive Computation Budgets for Real-Time Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23662v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Planning while moving faces an historical trade-off between deliberation and reactivity. A planner running concurrently with execution needs time to compute, so the robot must commit to an open-loop action chunk for that duration: a longer commitment buys a better plan at the cost of feedback frequency. Existing controllers fix this action commitment, and consequently the computation budget, before deployment. However, the optimal computation time is highly state-dependent (due to factors such as unmodeled dynamics, safety constraints, and marginal optimisation gains), i.e. dynamic, therefore offline static tuning risks leaving systems blind during critical maneuvers or unable to find global effective solutions. We cast the identification of the optimal computation budget as a semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) in which a single decision defines both the robot action commitments and the time allocated to the solver. To solve this, we train a reinforcement-learning policy conditioned on the current state, the executing plan, and inexpensive planner statistics. Across ground-robot navigation, agile quadrotor flight, and contact-rich manipulation, the learned policy changes its budget with the demands of the scenario, discovering efficient reasoning patterns that outperform fixed-budget baselines. https://lorenzomagnino.github.io/learning-robot-metareasoning-review/</p></details> |  |
| **[EDAR: Learning Environment-Dependent Action Representations for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11427v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning effective action representations is critical for robotic manipulation, where raw control trajectories are often noisy, redundant, and difficult to model directly. Existing methods mainly encode the structure of the action stream itself, treating the role of actions in the environment as implicit. Yet manipulation is about changing the world: the same action segment can induce different outcomes under different scene contexts, making action semantics inherently environment-dependent. We propose EDAR, an Environment-Dependent Action Representation that grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences. By coupling motor commands with their environment-conditioned effects, EDAR encourages the learned action space to capture interaction semantics rather than merely command-level patterns. Experiments on simulated and real-robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that EDAR improves downstream policy learning, especially in long-horizon manipulation. These results highlight the importance of grounding action representations in executable control structure and environment-conditioned visual change.</p></details> |  |
| **[HexaShell: A Thin Harness for Exploring the Limits of VLM Agents as Robot Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23479v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on vision language models (VLMs) now compete with learned policies on robot manipulation leaderboards. We ask how far a coding agent can go when it controls a robot instead of a computer, and where it stops. Our system, HexaShell, keeps the harness thin: it only turns the agent's commands into robot motion. The agent drives a robot with two arms through one command line. It sees three RGB cameras and knows their calibration. After each failed attempt, the agent reads the log, finds the cause, and writes or fixes small programs called tools. In this way it keeps what it learns as code, as a coding agent does when it fixes a failing test. The tools may use only what the robot observes. They use triangulation to find where objects are, relative to each other and to the gripper. If two cameras see the same point, the point lies where the two camera rays meet. If a point lies on a known plane, such as the table or the top of a block, it lies where its ray meets that plane. When the head camera is not enough, the robot moves a wrist camera to get a second view. We build the tools on only ten layouts of one seed per task, then freeze them and test them on ten new layouts of the same seed. We use Codex with GPT-6-Astra at medium reasoning effort. On all 42 RoboDojo tasks, HexaShell reaches a success rate of 39.7% and a progress score of 48.0, averaged in the same way as the leaderboard. This is a new state of the art on RoboDojo. The limit is contact, not reasoning: pushing reaches 92.5% and memory tasks 65.0%, but tight insertion only 8.3%. When the agent fails, it usually sees the failure but cannot make the motion work. We open source the tools evolved so far at https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell</p></details> |
| **[A Tendon-Driven Robotic Jellyfish with Constrained Soft Actuation and Depth Control via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29132v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Jellyfish-inspired robots offer a compliant and efficient approach to underwater locomotion, but achieving large deformation together with repeatable actuation and closed-loop control remains challenging. In this work, we present a tendon-driven robotic jellyfish with constrained soft actuation. Each actuator combines a flexible substrate with discrete constraints, enabling bending up to \(150^\circ\) with an approximately linear tendon displacement-bending relationship. Eight actuators driven by four servos allow the robot to perform stable swimming, attitude adjustment, and self-righting. Based on the linear actuation, a reinforcement-learning controller is further developed, enabling closed-loop depth regulation in both simulation and physical experiments. These results show that mechanical constraints can improve the controllability of soft actuation while preserving compliant jellyfish-like motion, providing a route toward manoeuvrable and autonomous jellyfish robots.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 8 figures. Corrected a typo in an author name in the arXiv metadata. No changes to the manuscript</p></details> |
| **[AWAM: Active Trajectory-Conditioned View Imagination for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23396v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rapid progress in embodied intelligence has enabled increasingly capable robots to follow language instructions and manipulate diverse objects. However, strong results in structured environments do not automatically transfer to open-world conditions, where clutter, occlusion, and changing viewpoints can obscure the spatial relationships required for action. To address this perceptual gap, a virtual active-perception paradigm is introduced. AWAM, an Active World-Action Model actively plans where to observe and imagines what will be seen along the planned trajectory before acting. Task-related virtual camera motion is predicted and used as a structured query into a geometry-conditioned world model, producing a reusable spatial prior for manipulation, which is later compressed into a fixed memory and fused with live observations through cross-attention for diffusion-based action prediction. AWAM achieves competitive success rates with 75.9% on LIBERO-Occ, 62.4% on LIBERO-Plus, 76.5% on RLBench-OG Occlusion1, and 94.4% in real-robot trials. These results support improved robustness to the evaluated occlusions and distribution shifts, addressing part of the gap between structured manipulation and less controlled environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Enabling Tidying Tasks in Robotic Vacuum Cleaners via a Geometry-Induced Self-Locking Deployable Linkage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20150v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic vacuum cleaners must extend their functionality within tight limits on size, cost, and durability. Deployable linkages with few actuators meet these limits, but their backdrivability makes it difficult to hold a load-bearing posture without continuous motor torque. We introduce a two-stage deployable linkage that locks through its own geometry. At full deployment, an internal three-link subchain approaches a collinear, near-singular configuration in which the input-output transmission ratio tends to zero; plate loads therefore leave only a small residual back-driving torque, which drive-train friction and a lightweight magnetic detent absorb. A single actuator performs deployment and locking, a passive chassis-mounted trigger releases the lock for retraction, and a second actuator adjusts only the deployment height. We integrate the mechanism into a commercial robotic vacuum cleaner and validate it through a 100-cycle repeatability test, a quasi-static load test in which the locked plate held 50 N with the motor de-energized, and 140 pushing-type tidying trials on seven household objects against a commercial arm-equipped vacuum robot. We derive the analytical holding condition and discuss its limits as an intermediate design point between passive compliance and articulated manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 6 figures, 1 table. v2: corrected author name, added minor revisions</p></details> |
| **[Analytic Robot Self-Model: Learning Screw-Axis Kinematics for Rendering and Model-Based Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15558v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual self-modelling lets robots learn body models from observations, but such models usually encode kinematics implicitly rather than exposing metric joint parameters for computation and control. Moreover, a good fit to images does not by itself establish accurate kinematics. Here we present an explicit analytic robot self-model that requires neither deformation networks nor URDF/CAD templates. Multi-view images and joint readings establish Gaussian appearance and provide motion evidence for rigid link assignments, whereas sparse end-effector poses independently identify metric screw axes. The resulting closed-form forward kinematics and analytic Jacobian can be evaluated beyond the fitting ranges. We validate the model on a simulated seven-axis arm, a simulated twelve-joint quadruped and a real six-axis arm. In simulation, it achieves millimetre-level reaching and pick-and-place, as well as closed-loop quadruped walking. On the real arm, it renders held-out configurations, predicts end-effector positions with a median error of 6.4 mm, compared with 11.7 mm for a calibrated nominal model, and supports reaching with external marker feedback. A controlled comparison shows that image fit alone is not enough: two Panda models differing only in their joint axes are 0.55 dB apart in PSNR, yet their end-effector prediction errors differ 13.7-fold.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 6 figures, supplementary information included</p></details> |
| **[From Sliding Windows to Self-evolving Lifelong Memory: A Dual-perspective Survey of Memory Paradigms for Long-Horizon Robotics VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23319v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon robotic tasks expose a fundamental limitation of current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models because their restricted temporal context pre-vents effective accumulation and reuse of past experiences. As a result, memory has become a fundamental component for enabling embodied agents to perform long-horizon reasoning and adaptive decision-making. This survey presents a systematic taxonomy of VLA memory mechanisms based on memory scope and persistence, categorizing existing approaches into five representative paradigms and one emerging frontier. Based on this taxonomy, we compare representative VLA memory systems in terms of memory characteristics, architectural com-plexity, and open-source maturity, revealing a clear trade-off in which increasing architectural complexity is generally accompanied by decreasing open-source ma-turity. Finally, we discuss the key challenges in building full-lifecycle memory systems, including efficient memory management and lifelong knowledge evolu-tion. Overall, this survey provides a unified perspective on memory architectures for VLA agents and offers insights into the development of lifelong embodied in-telligence.</p></details> | 10 pages, 1 figure |
| **[CoEvolve: Self-Evolving Code Policies from Skills to Strategies for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23288v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World Action Models (WAMs) have expanded the manipulation and whole body capabilities of individual robots. Yet a single robot remains limited by its embodiment, and many real world tasks require capabilities that can only emerge through cooperation. Existing multi robot frameworks largely emphasize task decomposition, allocation, and replanning, while providing limited support for adapting executable coordination when physical execution deviates from the intended behavior. We present CoEvolve, a framework that represents team coordination as executable code over fixed robot motor skills. CoEvolve introduces Verifier Guided Coordination Evolution, where a multimodal foundation model instantiates coordination strategies through motion primitives, semantic objectives, temporary robot roles, and bounded execution parameters. Physical verification evaluates each executed transition, rejected behaviors trigger structured coordination revision from an accepted checkpoint, and verified experience is retained to guide subsequent decisions. We evaluate CoEvolve with two simulated Unitree G1 humanoids transporting a freely supported rigid payload through constrained corridors. Across 15 base task runs with three multimodal backbone families, CoEvolve completes 8 tasks, with every backbone achieving end to end success. Additional evaluations yield verified placement on two additional corridor layouts and after both same-backbone and cross-backbone experience initialization, while exposing persistent limitations in motion precision and contact stability. These results support executable coordination as a practical interface for adapting long horizon multi robot collaboration under shared physical constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under review at ICLR 2027</p></details> |
| **[NativeMEM: Native Memory Compression for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06678v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How can pretrained Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models retain long-horizon visual histories with high-frequency updates without sacrificing efficiency? To this end, we present NativeMEM, a VLA policy that features long-term and real-time updated memory. At its core is an efficient memory encoding scheme, Native Memory Compression, which repurposes the VLA's own vision encoder to compress each historical frame from each camera view into a single token. Appended to the input sequence, these memory tokens enable the pretrained VLA to attend over long-term history with negligible latency overhead, requiring neither an external planner nor a freshly initialized memory module. To align the memory tokens with the pretrained policy, we first develop a generic memory tokenizer under the supervision of a frozen VLA on memory-demanding data, and then unfreeze the VLA for task-specific fine-tuning. NativeMEM consistently outperforms prior methods, boosting success rates from 32.4% to 84.0% in simulation and up to 98.7% on real robots, while maintaining low inference latency and GPU memory usage. Notably, NativeMEM exhibits high data efficiency by achieving competitive results with prior arts using only 20% of the training data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by CoRL 2026. Project Page: https://opendrivelab.com/NativeMEM</p></details> |
| **[Perceptive Behavior Foundation Model: Adapting Human Motion Priors to Robot-Centric Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08059v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humanoid behavior foundation models aim to acquire reusable whole-body control policies from broad human motion priors, enabling a single controller to produce diverse and expressive behaviors. However, existing motion-centric foundation policies largely assume that the reference motion is already physically compatible with the robot's surroundings. This assumption breaks when the demonstrator, operator, and robot inhabit different environments: a human motion may specify the intended behavior, but not the footholds, clearance, body height, or contact timing required by the robot's local terrain. We introduce \emph{Perceptive Behavior Foundation Model} (Perceptive BFM), a terrain-aware humanoid control framework that grounds human motion priors in robot-centric perception. The model preserves raw kinematic motion references as the behavioral interface, while using local terrain observations to adapt contacts, posture, and timing. To provide scalable terrain supervision, we develop \emph{terrain-conformal reference synthesis} (TCRS), which converts locomotion-oriented human motion clips into terrain-consistent references through contact-aware foothold construction, foot-geometry-aware swing optimization, support-aware root reconstruction, collision repair, and multi-point inverse kinematics. We then train a blind adapted-reference teacher and transfer its terrain-conformal behavior to a deployed raw-reference student through target-frame action alignment. The student is an identity-gated Transformer tracker whose terrain features enter through residual pathways initialized to preserve the motion-tracking prior and trained to produce local corrections only when needed.</p></details> |  |
| **[ORD-WM: Learning Object-Centric Rigid Dynamics for 3D World Modeling in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23156v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models provide robots with a predictive mechanism for reasoning about how actions change object states in manipulation tasks. To address this, we propose ORD-WM, a 3D world model that learns object-centric rigid dynamics for robotic manipulation. ORD-WM learns a vector-neuron variational autoencoder supervised by signed distance fields, which encodes each object point cloud as a geometry-aware latent representation composed of 3D vector features, and uses an action-conditioned dynamics model to predict object-wise SE(3) motion increments. Rather than generating unconstrained next latent states, ORD-WM analytically applies predicted object-wise rigid transformations to the current latent states, separating persistent object geometry from pose changes. Experiments show that ORD-WM enables stable, geometry-preserving multi-step prediction and supports efficient real-time replanning for closed-loop model predictive control in simulated and real-world manipulation scenarios.</p></details> |  |
| **[GRASP: Global-Local Representation Alignment and Spatial-Physical Grounding for Bimanual Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23123v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models that directly regress joint-space actions avoid the deployment-time cost of inverse kinematics. First, a physics-grounded flow-matching loss maps the implicit clean-action estimate at every noise level through differentiable forward kinematics (FK) and supervises the resulting Tool Center Point (TCP) pose and inter-arm relative pose, with a bounded noise-level weighting that compensates gradient decay. Both teachers are removed at inference, and the deployment pipeline is that of a standard joint-space flow policy, adding only the learned query pathway. The full model excels in in-distribution deployment, while the distilled query branch is preferable under strong visual randomization.</p></details> | 8 pages, 4 figures |
| **[InstAgent: A Recursively Self-Improving Agent Operating System for Heterogeneous Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23098v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous robot teams enable complex, long-horizon tasks by combining complementary sensing, mobility, and manipulation capabilities across multiple embodiments. Yet existing systems struggle to retain execution-time corrections as reusable experience, maintain task-relevant context memory across space and time for reliable handoffs, and explicitly represent the inter-robot dependencies required for structured collaboration. In order to address these challenges, we present InstAgent, a recursively self-improving (RSI) Agent Operating System for heterogeneous robot collaboration. InstAgent organizes team behaviors as agentic behavior trees (BTs) that explicitly model capability assignment, inter-robot dependencies and handoff checks. The RSI mechanism updates these collaboration programs from execution feedback and preserves successful revisions for reuse in future tasks, while shared multimodal spatio-temporal memory provides context for planning and handoff validation. Experiments on 20 real-world multi-robot tasks with three high-level models show consistent gains in task success, first-attempt completion, and execution efficiency.</p></details> | 9 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Foundation Models for Autonomous Space Robotics: Architectures, Physical Adaptation, and Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23068v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Space robots must interpret tasks and act despite unfamiliar dynamics, difficult sensing conditions, and limited opportunities for human intervention. Foundation models offer reusable perceptual and behavioral knowledge, but their value depends on how that knowledge reaches physical execution. This critical review follows space-specific requirements through perception, prediction, planning, and action to deployment and validation, emphasizing orbital manipulation and proximity operations. Terrestrial comparisons establish benefits of particular pretraining stages and model interfaces; their transfer to space dynamics and constrained onboard operation remains insufficiently tested. Event cameras offer timely motion observations across strong illumination contrasts, while spiking neural networks provide possible routes to sparse local computation. We examine how sensor degradation, state estimation and computing choices affect feedback and recovery. Higher action throughput need not yield fresher feedback; lower processor power can also coexist with higher energy per inference. We organize validation as an iterative sim-to-ground-to-space pathway, connecting physical tests with telemetry-based diagnosis and closed-loop revalidation. A satellite-capture example links observations to approach, contact and recovery, while a corresponding protocol separates generalization from adaptation with limited target-domain interaction. These comparisons identify what models already contribute and the physical evidence needed to support autonomous space operation.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 7 figures. Critical review. Ancillary files include source inventory and evidence documentation</p></details> |
| **[Robotic Spatiotemporal Editing: A Unified Interface for Controllable Action Generation via Masked Action Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23002v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative policies can synthesize robot behaviors, yet controlling and editing them through diverse spatiotemporal references remains challenging. | **[Towards Robot Skill Learning and Adaptation with Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01480v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General robot skill adaptation requires expressive representations robust to varying task configurations. We present three adaptation methods to cater for the variability between initial and observed configurations. Firstly, an optimisation agent that adjusts the path's via-points while preserving the demonstration velocity. Second, a behaviour cloning agent trained to replicate output trajectories from the optimisation agent. Lastly, an RL agent that has learnt to modify via-points whilst maintaining the kinematic profile and enabling online capabilities. Furthermore, the results demonstrate that the GP-based representation enables all three methods to attain high cosine similarity and low velocity magnitude errors, indicating strong preservation of the kinematic profile. Overall, our formulation provides a compact representation capable of adapting to large deviations from a single demonstrated skill.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters (RA-L), 2026. | **[Pedicel-Targeted Strawberry Harvesting via Imitation Learning on a Low-Cost Teleoperated Robotic Arm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22765v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Precise pedicel-targeted grasping in strawberry harvesting remains a critical bottleneck due to the pedicel's small diameter (1.3-3 mm), color similarity with surrounding foliage, and precise force control required for clean abscission zone detachment. We have also established a reproducible open-source pipeline with publicly released dataset and model weights for the agricultural robotics community. Full dataset is available at, https://huggingface.co/datasets/nitindominicrai/strawberry_pedicel_grasp_teleoperation and trained policy at, https://huggingface.co/nitindominicrai/act_strawberry_pedicel.
