This paper was peer-reviewed and accepted in the IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), "From Innovation to Adoption: Hardware, AI, Policy, and Funding Challenges in Agricultural Robotics Workshop," Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Robots for Everyday Environments: From Lab Experiments to Real-World Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26490v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study presents an interdisciplinary process for benchmarking service robots in public everyday environments, addressing the gap between laboratory metrics and real-world operation at technology readiness levels 6-7. Three robots were evaluated in three use cases: outdoor park cleaning, pedestrian underpass cleaning, and interactive assistance in a public library. Over three years (2023-2025), seven benchmarking events (a consensus workshop and six on-site evaluations, two per use case) assessed the robots in increasingly realistic settings, from controlled conditions to public operation. An interdisciplinary panel of experts in robotics, human-robot interaction, safety, and economic viability consolidated use-case-specific metrics into a shared evaluation concept refined across three phases. Rather than ranking the robots, the evaluation examined how task fulfillment, interaction quality, safety, and economic viability interact in the transition from laboratory prototype to operational system. Direct quantitative comparability across the heterogeneous use cases remained limited, characteristic of benchmarking at this maturity level. The process yielded cross-case insights: increasing environmental realism introduces challenges that uniform metrics capture only in part; the interaction-quality guideline works best as a modular instrument adapted to each use case; safety was assessed by occupational-safety and product-certification experts who adapted industrial test procedures for public settings; and economic viability proved a cross-cutting dimension. We conclude that benchmarking at higher technology readiness levels is well served by a shared procedure for selecting and documenting metrics across these four categories, with safety as mandatory, rather than by a fixed set of metrics: generalizability lies in the procedure rather than in identical metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 14 figures, 5 tables. Accepted for publication in Frontiers in Robotics and AI (Field Robotics)</p></details> |
| **[Task Structure and Scalability in Multi-Robot Authoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22670v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-robot authoring can reduce completion time by distributing drawing work, but the resulting speedup depends on task structure and the allocation-routing pipeline. | **[Monkey See, Can Monkey Do? | **[IDfRA: Self-Verification for Iterative Design in Robotic Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16998v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Design for Robotic Assembly (DfRA) remains largely dependent on manual planning and heuristic simulation, limiting scalability and robustness in complex industrial settings. We introduce Iterative Design for Robotic Assembly (IDfRA), a closed-loop framework that combines an LLM for plan generation with a vision-language model (VLM) for execution assessment. Given a target structure and a partial environmental signature, the LLM proposes an assembly plan, the robot executes it once at test time, and the VLM evaluates the resulting state to provide feedback for replanning. Through this iterative planning-execution-verification loop, the system progressively improves semantic fidelity and physical feasibility. Crucially, IDfRA does not require an accurate a priori world model before deployment. Instead, physical constraints are discovered online through interaction, enabling adaptation to under-specified environments. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that IDfRA attains 73.3% top-1 accuracy in semantic recognisability, surpassing the baseline on this metric. Moreover, the resulting assembly plans exhibit robust physical feasibility, achieving an overall 86.9% construction success rate, with design quality improving across iterations, albeit not always monotonically. Pairwise human evaluation further corroborates the advantages of IDfRA relative to alternative approaches. By integrating self-verification with context-aware adaptation, the framework evidences strong potential for deployment in unstructured manufacturing scenarios.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 8 figures. Published at ICRA 2026</p></details> |
| **[BubbleCBF: Constructive Safety for Second-Order Systems and Application to Autonomous Robot Navigation in Unknown Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20895v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Satisfying safety constraints for systems with second-order dynamics is challenging because a position constraint may require infeasible deceleration. Starting from a signed distance function (SDF), we construct collision-free spheres, i.e., bubbles, in position space, and use their quadratic geometry and overlap to construct a velocity-dependent safety constraint. The associated safe set is represented as a union of intersections and enforced through a quadratic-program safety filter. Under mild assumptions on the dynamics, bubble overlap, and design parameters, we prove that the quadratic-program-based controller is well defined, continuous in the system state, and renders the set forward invariant. We also derive sufficient conditions for feasibility under bounded inputs. We demonstrate that our BubbleCBF, applied to a differential-drive robot, achieves safe autonomous navigation in unknown environments. Videos and results are available at: https://existentialrobotics.org/bubblecbf/.</p></details> |  |
| **[Optimally Trading Energy for Information through Speed Trajectory Optimization of Sustainably Powered Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20875v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sustainably powered mobile robots enable long-duration observational missions by harvesting energy from the environment. However, effective autonomy requires the robots to make decisions as to how to allocate this energy to maximize information quality over time. In this paper, we study a persistent (infinite-horizon) speed trajectory optimization problem that is posed as a periodic optimal control problem in which a robot repeatedly traverses a fixed closed path, coupling a super-linear propulsion cost with dynamic information gain and decay. We then derive a number of specialized follow-on properties, which characterize (i) the nature of the optimal control when points on the path are of varying importance and when they are of equal importance, along with (ii) a high-speed information limit that is approached when energy reserves approach infinity. We illustrate the information-optimal speed trajectories on a simple example in order to showcase key trends that are followed under the optimized speed profile. This is followed by a simulation study based on the persistent characterization of salinity in the Chesapeake Bay, illustrating the practical merit of applying the results from this work.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robotic-supported Data Loss Detection in Android Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20807v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Smartphones have become integral to modern life due to their diverse applications. In this context, robotic automation has emerged as an effective solution for addressing the diversity of smartphone systems and versions, and in performing some testing tasks non-invasively. In addition, automated robots facilitate quick, accurate, and repetitive testing, thus enabling developers to validate their applications across various configurations effectively. This results in a less invasive verification and in a significant reduction in time spent on manual testing, thus accelerating the development cycle. Our work proposes R-DLD (Robotic-supported Data Loss Detection), a robot-assisted infrastructure for data loss detection in Android applications, offeringless invasive and more realistic tests by interacting directly with smartphone sensors. The robot is constructed using cost-effective materials, facilitating its adoption in testing environments. Finally, we use feature-based data attribution to isolate where a specific behavior (failure recovery) arises in the training dataset, identifying fewer than $1.4\%$ of training tokens whose masking is sufficient to remove the targeted behavior. The content of this information changes with token and layer within the model. Early-to-mid layer representations of the target noun token determine which object is selected, whereas mid-to-late layers carry the spatial information in text tokens that redirects the movement when patched. Where spatial information is stored in prompt tokens various based on the architecture. | **[EarlyCommit: Calibrated Word-by-Word Commitment for Robots Acting Before You Finish Speaking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20531v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human listeners can often identify the intended object several words before an instruction ends. These experiments show that successful timing depends on both measured intervals and retained history. TFP achieves higher average success than the recurrent and state-space memories we equip with the same intervals and than a variant that feeds the interval to a learned gate. Memory readouts recover the time since an event and motion information after occlusion. Changing the supplied intervals systematically delays or advances actions, linking time inputs to behavior. | **[What the Elevation Map Cannot See: Semantic-Aware Locomotion and Execution-Aware Navigation for Humanoid Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07396v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Navigation for humanoid robots is critical, yet large-scale evaluation on physical hardware is often impractical due to cost and safety concerns, making simulation benchmarks essential. | **[Towards Agentic Robots: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20442v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models are driving a paradigm shift in robotic intelligence, moving beyond low-level action prediction to goal-directed orchestration of data collection, model training, task planning, and real-world execution. We first trace the development from robot policies to agentic systems, covering vision-language-action models, world models, and world-action models. We then clarify the definition and scope of agentic robots and organize existing research into seventeen categories across four functional domains: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness. Across these domains, we examine how agents shape model learning and robot decisions, collect and curate robot data, construct worlds, tasks, and tests for learning and evaluation, and organize capabilities for robot use. Finally, we discuss open challenges and outline directions for future research. | **[X-Safe: Embodiment-Agnostic Safeguarding for Robot Arm Manipulation Policies via Action Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22278v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ensuring safety of learning-enabled robotic manipulation across diverse embodiments and tasks still requires significant manual engineering. | **[Human Demonstrations as Post-Training Data for Any Robot Body: Retargeting the Object, Not the Hand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20351v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human demonstrations are today used largely for pretraining vision-language-action models, with little ability to use the same data for target-embodiment or task-oriented training, which scalable deployment of robots in industrial and general environments depends on. This is made possible not by retargeting the hand's motion but the object's. A capture yields a body-free record: the object's pose over time, its contact intervals, and a reconstructed 3D object with provenance. Any robot, given as a URDF and a short descriptor, is solved against that record and scored in simulation against the demonstrated object motion as ground truth: the human-to-simulation leg of human-to-simulation-to-robot transfer, measured per robot. Each (robot, episode) pair gets one outcome: pass, failure with a named mode, or refusal, with its reason, before any motion. The method runs as a working system over a growing collection; the set analysed here is a sample of it: 42 captures solved on 8 robots, 157 pairs, of which 31.2% pass, 52.2% fail, 14.0% are refused and 2.5% are unauditable (kinematic feasibility, +/-50 mm / +/-15 deg, in simulation). Refusals held up: every pair the checks refuse on stored evidence yet ran anyway failed (13 of 13; 5 on a body whose public model welds its hand). A description file could not be shown to screen a robot: its outcomes were barely more predictable than guessing. No policy has yet been trained on the record. The record decides what a robot may post-train on; whether the policy then works on the real robot is the simulation-to-robot question, answered downstream.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 17 figures, 7 tables, 2 algorithms; 56-page supplement as ancillary file. A subset of episodes with their per-robot outcomes: https://playground.dxtr.co</p></details> |

Our result settles an open question of Agarwal, Halperin, Sharir, Steiger [SODA'24] and Agarwal, Holmgren, Steiger [SODA'26], who gave an FPTAS for $k=2$ unit-square robots and a bicriteria-approximation algorithm for a fixed number $k$ of unit-square or unit-disk robots, respectively. | **[MUCO: A Mani-Unit-Structured Manipulation Framework for Compositional Long-Horizon Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19913v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning and executing long-horizon robot manipulation as complete task trajectories raises data-collection costs, lim?its capability reuse, and causes local failures to cascade into later task stages.
